\section{SpecCT: Cryptographic Constant-Time and Speculative Constant-Time}

This chapter starts by presenting the cryptographic constant-time (CCT)
    discipline, which we statically enforce using a simple type system. This
    static discipline is, however, not enough to protect cryptographic programs
    against speculative execution attacks. To secure CCT programs against this
    more powerful attacker model we additionally use a program transformation
    called \textit{Speculative Load Hardening} (SLH). We prove formally that CCT
    programs protected by SLH achieve speculative constant-time security. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\section{Cryptographic constant-time}



 Cryptographic constant-time (CCT) is a software countermeasure against
    timing side-channel attacks that is widely deployed for cryptographic
    implementations, for instance to prevent leakage of crypto keys
    \coqdocvar{Barthe} \coqdocvar{et} \coqdocvar{al} 2019 (in Bib.v).


    More generally, each program input has to be identified as public or secret,
    and intuitively the execution time of the program should not depend on
    secret inputs, even on processors with instruction and data caches.


    We, however, do not want to explicitly model execution time or caches, since

\begin{itemize}
\item  it would be very hard to do right, and

\item  it would bring in too many extremely low-level details of the concrete compiler
      (Clang/LLVM 20.1.6) and hardware microarchitecture (Intel Core i7-8650U). 
\end{itemize}


 Instead CCT works with a \textit{more abstract model of leakage},
    which simply assumes that:

\begin{itemize}
\item  \textit{all branches the program takes are leaked};

\begin{itemize}
\item  since the path the program takes can greatly
        influence how long execution takes

\item  this is exactly like in the Control Flow (CF)
        security model from \coqdoclibrary{StaticIFC}

\end{itemize}

\item  \textit{all accessed memory addresses are leaked};

\begin{itemize}
\item  since timing attacks can also exploit the latency difference between
        hits and misses in the data cache

\end{itemize}

\item  \textit{the operands influencing timing of variable-time operations are leaked};

\begin{itemize}
\item  as an exercise we will add a division operation that leaks both operands.

\end{itemize}

\end{itemize}


 To ensure security against this leakage model, the CCT discipline requires that:



\begin{itemize}
\item  \textit{the control flow of the program does not depend on secrets};

\begin{itemize}
\item  intuitively this prevents the execution time of different program paths
        from directly depending on secrets:


        if Wsecret then ... slow computation ... else skip



\end{itemize}

\item  \textit{the accessed memory addresses do not depend on secrets};

\begin{itemize}
\item  intuitively this prevents secrets from leaking into the data cache:


        Vsecret <- AP\coqdocvar{Wsecret}



\end{itemize}

\item  \textit{the operands leaked by variable-time operations do not depend on secrets}.

\begin{itemize}
\item  this prevents leaking information about secrets e.g., via division:


        Usecret := div Vsecret Wsecret

\end{itemize}

\end{itemize}


 To model memory accesses that depend on secrets we will make the Imp
    language more realistic by adding arrays. 

 We need such an extension, since
    otherwise variable accesses in the original Imp map to memory operations at
    constant locations, which thus cannot depend on secrets, so in Imp CCT
    trivially holds for all CF well-typed programs. Array indices on the other
    hand are computed at runtime, which leads to accessing memory addresses that
    can depend on secrets, making CCT non-trivial for Imp with arrays.


    For instance, here is a simple program that is CF secure (since it does no
    branches), but not CCT secure (since it accesses memory based on secret
    information):

\begin{itemize}
\item  \coqdocvar{Vsecret} \ensuremath{\leftarrow} \coqdocvar{A}[\coqdocvar{Wsecret}]  

\end{itemize}
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{Adding constant-time conditional and refactoring expressions}



 But first, we add a \coqdocvar{b} ? \coqdocvar{e1} : \coqdocvar{e2} conditional expression that executes in
    constant time (for instance by being compiled to a special constant-time
    conditional move instruction). This constant-time conditional will also be
    used in our SLH countermeasure below. 

 Technically, adding such conditionals to Imp arithmetic expressions would
    make them dependent on boolean expressions. But boolean expressions are
    already dependent on arithmetic expressions. 

 To avoid making the definitions of arithmetic and boolean expressions
    mutually inductive, we drop boolean expressions altogether and encode them
    using arithmetic expressions. Our encoding of bools in terms of nats is
    similar to that of C, where zero means false, and non-zero means true. 

 We also refactor the semantics of binary operators in terms of the
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.binop}{\coqdocinductive{binop}} enumeration below, to avoid the duplication in Imp: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Inductive} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.binop}{binop}{\coqdocinductive{binop}} : \coqdockw{Type} :=\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.BinPlus}{BinPlus}{\coqdocconstructor{BinPlus}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.BinMinus}{BinMinus}{\coqdocconstructor{BinMinus}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.BinMult}{BinMult}{\coqdocconstructor{BinMult}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.BinEq}{BinEq}{\coqdocconstructor{BinEq}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.BinLe}{BinLe}{\coqdocconstructor{BinLe}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.BinAnd}{BinAnd}{\coqdocconstructor{BinAnd}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.BinImpl}{BinImpl}{\coqdocconstructor{BinImpl}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
We define the semantics of \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.binop}{\coqdocinductive{binop}}s directly on nats. We are careful to
    allow other representations of true (any non-zero number).  
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Definition} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.not zero}{not\_zero}{\coqdocdefinition{not\_zero}} (\coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.n:3}{n}{\coqdocbinder{n}} : \coqexternalref{nat}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{nat}}) : \coqexternalref{bool}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{bool}} := \coqexternalref{negb}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocdefinition{negb}} (\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.n:3}{\coqdocvariable{n}} \coqexternalref{::nat scope:x '=?' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Nat}{\coqdocnotation{=?}} 0).\coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Definition} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.bool to nat}{bool\_to\_nat}{\coqdocdefinition{bool\_to\_nat}} (\coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.b:4}{b}{\coqdocbinder{b}} : \coqexternalref{bool}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{bool}}) : \coqexternalref{nat}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{nat}} := \coqdockw{if} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.b:4}{\coqdocvariable{b}} \coqdockw{then} 1 \coqdockw{else} 0.\coqdoceol

\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\coqdockw{match} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.o:5}{\coqdocvariable{o}} \coqdockw{with}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\ensuremath{|} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.BinPlus}{\coqdocconstructor{BinPlus}} \ensuremath{\Rightarrow} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.n1:6}{\coqdocvariable{n1}} \coqexternalref{::nat scope:x '+' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Nat}{\coqdocnotation{+}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.n2:7}{\coqdocvariable{n2}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\ensuremath{|} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.BinMinus}{\coqdocconstructor{BinMinus}} \ensuremath{\Rightarrow} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.n1:6}{\coqdocvariable{n1}} \coqexternalref{::nat scope:x '-' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Nat}{\coqdocnotation{-}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.n2:7}{\coqdocvariable{n2}}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\ensuremath{|} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.BinMult}{\coqdocconstructor{BinMult}} \ensuremath{\Rightarrow} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.n1:6}{\coqdocvariable{n1}} \coqexternalref{::nat scope:x '*' x}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Nat}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\times}}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.n2:7}{\coqdocvariable{n2}}\coqdoceol

The notations we use for expressions are the same as in Imp,
    except the notation for \coqdocvar{be}?\coqdocvar{e1}:\coqdocvar{e2} which is new:

\subsection{Instrumenting semantics with observations}



 In addition to the boolean branches, which are observable in the CF security
    model, for CCT security also the index of array loads and stores are
    observable: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Inductive} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.observation}{observation}{\coqdocinductive{observation}} : \coqdockw{Type} :=\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.OBranch}{OBranch}{\coqdocconstructor{OBranch}} (\coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.b:62}{b}{\coqdocbinder{b}} : \coqexternalref{bool}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{bool}})\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.OALoad}{OALoad}{\coqdocconstructor{OALoad}} (\coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.a:63}{a}{\coqdocbinder{a}} : \coqdocinductive{string}) (\coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.i:64}{i}{\coqdocbinder{i}} : \coqexternalref{nat}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{nat}})\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.00em}
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.OAStore}{OAStore}{\coqdocconstructor{OAStore}} (\coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.a:65}{a}{\coqdocbinder{a}} : \coqdocinductive{string}) (\coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.i:66}{i}{\coqdocbinder{i}} : \coqexternalref{nat}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{nat}}).\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Definition} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.obs}{obs}{\coqdocdefinition{obs}} := \coqexternalref{list}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{list}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.observation}{\coqdocinductive{observation}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
We define an instrumented big-step operational semantics producing these
    observations:

\begin{itemize}
\item  <(\coqdocvar{st}, \coqdocvar{m})> =[ \coqdocvar{c} ]=> <(\coqdocvar{st'}, \coqdocvar{m'}, \coqdocvar{os})>

\end{itemize}


    Intuitively, variables act like registers (not observable),
    while arrays act like the memory (addresses observable). 

\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}     (CTE\_Skip)
          <(st, m)> = \coqdocvar{skip} => <(st, m, \ensuremath{\Box})>


                          eval st e = n
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}    (CTE\_Asgn)
      <(st, m)> = \coqdocvar{x} := \coqdocvar{e} => <(x !-> n; st, m, \ensuremath{\Box})>


            <(st, m)> = \coqdocvar{c1} => <(st', m', os1)>
            <(st', m')> = \coqdocvar{c2} => <(st'', m'', os2)>
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}   (CTE\_Seq)
      <(st, m)>  = \coqdocvar{c1} ; \coqdocvar{c2} => <(st'', m'', os1 ++ os2)>


       let c := if not\_zero (eval st be) then c1 else c2 in
            <(st,m)> = \coqdocvar{c} => <(st',m',os1)>
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{} (CTE\_If)
  <(st, m)> = \coqdockw{if} \coqdocvar{be} \coqdockw{then} \coqdocvar{c1} \coqdockw{else} \coqdocvar{c2} \coqdockw{end}=>
   <(st', m', \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.OBranch}{\coqdocconstructor{OBranch}} (\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.not zero}{\coqdocdefinition{not\_zero}} (\coqdoctac{eval} \coqdocvar{st} \coqdocvar{be})) ++ os1)>


<(st,m)> = \coqdockw{if} \coqdocvar{be} \coqdockw{then} \coqdocvar{c}; \coqdocvar{while} \coqdocvar{be} \coqdoctac{do} \coqdocvar{c} \coqdockw{end} \coqdockw{else} \coqdocvar{skip} \coqdockw{end} => <(st',m',os)>
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{} (CTE\_While)
            <(st,m)> = \coqdocvar{while} \coqdocvar{be} \coqdoctac{do} \coqdocvar{c} \coqdockw{end} => <(st', m', os)>


              eval st ie = i              i < length (m a)
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{} (CTE\_ALoad)
<(st,m)> = \coqdocvar{x} \ensuremath{\leftarrow} \coqdocvar{a}[[\coqdocvar{ie}]] => <(x!->nth i (m a) 0; st, m,\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.OALoad}{\coqdocconstructor{OALoad}} \coqdocvar{a} \coqdocvar{i})>


   eval st e = n     eval st ie = i    i < length (m a)
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}   (CTE\_AStore)
<(st,m)> = \coqdocvar{a}[\coqdocvar{ie}] \ensuremath{\leftarrow} \coqdocvar{e} => <(st, a!->upd i (m a) n; m,\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.OAStore}{\coqdocconstructor{OAStore}} \coqdocvar{a} \coqdocvar{i})>



\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Reserved Notation}\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{4.50em}
"'<(' st , m ')>' '=[' c ']=>' '<(' stt , mt , os ')>'"\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{4.50em}
(\coqdoctac{at} \coqdockw{level} 40, \coqdocvar{c} \coqdocvar{custom} \coqdocvar{com} \coqdoctac{at} \coqdockw{level} 99,\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{5.00em}

\subsection{Example CF secure program that is not CCT secure}


\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Definition} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.cct insecure prog}{cct\_insecure\_prog}{\coqdocdefinition{cct\_insecure\_prog}} :=\coqdoceol
\coqdocindent{1.50em}
\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.::com scope:'<x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D>'}{\coqdocnotation{<\{\{}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.V}{\coqdocdefinition{V}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.:com:com scope:x '<-' x '[[' x ']]'}{\coqdocnotation{\ensuremath{\leftarrow}}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.AP}{\coqdocdefinition{AP}}\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.:com:com scope:x '<-' x '[[' x ']]'}{\coqdocnotation{[[}}\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.W}{\coqdocdefinition{W}}\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.:com:com scope:x '<-' x '[[' x ']]'}{\coqdocnotation{]]}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.::com scope:'<x7Bx7B' x 'x7Dx7D>'}{\coqdocnotation{\}\}>}} .\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
Let's assume that \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.W}{\coqdocdefinition{W}} and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.V}{\coqdocdefinition{V}} are secret variables.
    This program is trivially CF secure, because it does not branch at all.
    But it is not CCT secure. 

 This is proved below. We first define the public variables and arrays, which
    we will use in this kind of examples:

\subsection{Type system for cryptographic constant-time programming}



 In our CCT type system, the label assigned to the result of a constant-time
    conditional expression simply joins the labels of the 3 involved expressions:


        P |- be \symbol{92}in l   P |- e1 \symbol{92}in l1    P |- e2 \symbol{92}in l2
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{} (T\_CTIf)
             P |- be?e1:e2 \symbol{92}in join l (join l1 l2)


    The rules for the other expressions are standard, and a lot fewer
    because of our refactoring:
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}   (T\_Num)
            P |- n \symbol{92}in public
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}   (T\_Id)
            P |- X \symbol{92}in (P X)


       P |- e1 \symbol{92}in l1      P |- e2 \symbol{92}in l2
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{}   (T\_Bin)
       P |- (e1 `op` e2) \symbol{92}in (join l1 l2)

\subsection{Exercise: CCT Type-Checker}



 In these exercises you will write a type-checker for the CCT type system
    above and prove your type-checker sound and complete. If you get stuck, you
    can take inspiration in the similar type-checkers from \coqdoclibrary{StaticIFC}.

 Finally, we use the type-checker to show that the \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.cct insecure prog}{\coqdocdefinition{cct\_insecure\_prog}} and
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.cct insecure prog'}{\coqdocdefinition{cct\_insecure\_prog'}} examples above are not well-typed. 
\begin{coqdoccode}

\coqdockw{Print} \coqdocvar{XYZpub}. \coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Print} \coqdocvar{APpub}. \coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}

\subsection{Noninterference lemma}



 To prove the security of our type system, we first show a noninterference
    lemma, which is not that hard, given that our very restrictive type system
    ensures the two executions run in lock-step, since it disallows branching
    on secrets. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Lemma} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.cct well typed noninterferent}{cct\_well\_typed\_noninterferent}{\coqdoclemma{cct\_well\_typed\_noninterferent}} :\coqdoceol

Most cases of this proof are similar to the security proof for
    \coqref{SECF.StaticIFC.cf well typed}{\coqdocinductive{cf\_well\_typed}} from \coqdoclibrary{StaticIFC}. In particular, \coqdocvar{noninterference} is
    used to prove the sequence case in both proofs.


    The only new cases here are for array operations, and they follow
    immediately from \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.noninterferent exp}{\coqdoclemma{noninterferent\_exp}}, since the CCT type system requires
    array indices to be public. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{Exercise: Adding division (non-constant-time operation)}



 The CCT discipline also prevents passing secrets to operations that are not
    constant time. For instance, division often takes time that depends on the
    values of the two operands. In this exercise we will add a new
    \coqdocvar{x} := \coqdocvar{e1} \coqdocvar{div} \coqdocvar{e2} command for division, add corresponding evaluation and
    typing rules, and extend the security proofs with the new division case.

We add a new rule to the big-step operational semantics that produces an
    \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.ODiv}{\coqdocconstructor{ODiv}} observation:


               eval st e1 = n1     eval st e2 = n2
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{} (CTE\_Div)
<(st,m)> =\coqdocvar{x} := \coqdocvar{e1} \coqdocvar{div} \coqdocvar{e2}=> <(x!->(n1/n2);st,m,\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.ODiv}{\coqdocconstructor{ODiv}} \coqdocvar{n1} \coqdocvar{n2})>

\section{Speculative constant-time (text under development)}



 This second part of the chapter is based on the Spectre
    Declassified paper \coqdocvar{Shivakumar} \coqdocvar{et} \coqdocvar{al} 2023 (in Bib.v) in simplified form
    (e.g., without declassification). Like in this paper, we only look
    at a class of speculative execution attacks called Spectre v1. 

 The Rocq development below is complete, but the text about it is still under
    development and gets sparse after the first 3-4 subsections, especially for
    the security proof. Readers can skip the security proof, or if they have
    access to the slides associated to this chapter (i.e. the TERSE version)
    look there for a high-level overview of the security proof. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{CCT programs can be insecure under speculative execution}



 All variables mentioned in the program below (\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}}, \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}}, \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.AP}{\coqdocdefinition{AP}}) are \textit{public},
    so this program respects the CCT discipline, yet this program is not secure
    under speculative execution. 

 The size of public array \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.AP}{\coqdocdefinition{AP}} is 3 and we check we're in bounds, yet this
    check can misspeculate! 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent

\coqdocindent{4.00em}

All variables mentioned in the program are again public, so also this
    program respects the CCT discipline, yet it is also not secure under
    speculative execution. 

 This example is formalized at the end of the chapter. 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
\subsection{Speculative semantics}



 To reason about the security of these examples against Spectre v1 we will
    introduce a speculative semantics. To model leakage the semantics uses the
    same CCT observations as above (\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.OBranch}{\coqdocconstructor{OBranch}}, \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.OALoad}{\coqdocconstructor{OALoad}}, and \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.OAStore}{\coqdocconstructor{OAStore}}). 

 More interestingly, to model speculative execution we add to the semantics
    adversary-provided \textit{directions}, which control the speculation behavior: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Inductive} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.direction}{direction}{\coqdocinductive{direction}} :=\coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.DStep}{DStep}{\coqdocconstructor{DStep}}  \coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.DForce}{DForce}{\coqdocconstructor{DForce}} \coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.DLoad}{DLoad}{\coqdocconstructor{DLoad}} (\coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.a:393}{a}{\coqdocbinder{a}} : \coqdocinductive{string}) (\coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.i:394}{i}{\coqdocbinder{i}} : \coqexternalref{nat}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{nat}})   \coqdoceol
\coqdocnoindent
\ensuremath{|} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.DStore}{DStore}{\coqdocconstructor{DStore}} (\coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.a:395}{a}{\coqdocbinder{a}} : \coqdocinductive{string}) (\coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.i:396}{i}{\coqdocbinder{i}} : \coqexternalref{nat}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{nat}}). \coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Definition} \coqdef{SECF.SpecCT.dirs}{dirs}{\coqdocdefinition{dirs}} := \coqexternalref{list}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocinductive{list}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.direction}{\coqdocinductive{direction}}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
This gives us a very high-level model of speculation that abstracts away
    low-level details such as the compiler, branch predictors, memory layout,
    speculation window, rollbacks, etc. We do this in a way that tries to
    overapproximate the adversary's power.


    This kind of speculation model is actually used by the Jasmin language for
    high-assurance crypto. 

 Compared to the CCT semantics with observations as output, we now add the
    directions as input to the evaluation judgement and we also track a
    misspeculation bit \coqdocvar{b}. 

\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{} (Spec\_Skip)
  <(st,m,b,\ensuremath{\Box})> =\coqdocvar{skip}=> <(st,m,b,\ensuremath{\Box})>


                 eval st e = n
\par
\noindent\hrulefill\par
\noindent{} (Spec\_Asgn)
   <(st,m,b,\ensuremath{\Box})> =\coqdocvar{x}:=\coqdocvar{e}=> <(x!->n;st,m,b,\ensuremath{\Box})>

\subsection{Speculative constant-time security definition}



 The definition of speculative constant-time security is very similar to CCT
    security, but applied to the speculative semantics. The two executions
    receive the same directions \coqdocvar{ds}:

We can use this definition to show that our first example is speculatively
    insecure: 
\begin{coqdoccode}
\coqdocemptyline
\coqdocnoindent
\coqdockw{Print} \coqdocvar{spec\_insecure\_prog}.\coqdoceol
\coqdocemptyline
\end{coqdoccode}
For this we build a counterexample where the attacker chooses an
    out-of-bounds index \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Y}{\coqdocdefinition{Y}} = 3 and then passes the directions:
    [\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.DForce}{\coqdocconstructor{DForce}}; \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.DLoad}{\coqdocconstructor{DLoad}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.AS}{\coqdocdefinition{AS}} 0; \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.DStep}{\coqdocconstructor{DStep}}].  This causes the two executions to load
    different values for \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.X}{\coqdocdefinition{X}} from index 0 of secret array \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.AS}{\coqdocdefinition{AS}}.
    If the different values loaded from \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.AS}{\coqdocdefinition{AS}}[0] are well chosen (e.g., 4 \ensuremath{\le} 5
    in the first execution and 7 > 5 in the second) this causes two different
    observations: - [\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.OBranch}{\coqdocconstructor{OBranch}} \coqexternalref{false}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocconstructor{false}}; \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.OALoad}{\coqdocconstructor{OALoad}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.AP}{\coqdocdefinition{AP}} 3; \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.OBranch}{\coqdocconstructor{OBranch}} \coqexternalref{true}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocconstructor{true}}] and - [\coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.OBranch}{\coqdocconstructor{OBranch}}
    \coqexternalref{false}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocconstructor{false}}; \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.OALoad}{\coqdocconstructor{OALoad}} \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.AP}{\coqdocdefinition{AP}} 3; \coqref{SECF.SpecCT.Div.OBranch}{\coqdocconstructor{OBranch}} \coqexternalref{false}{http://rocq-prover.org/doc/V9.1.1/stdlib//Corelib.Init.Datatypes}{\coqdocconstructor{false}}].

\paragraph{Exercise: 1 star, standard (speculation\_bit\_monotonic)}



 As mentioned above, our speculative semantics is very high-level, and
    doesn't have to deal with detecting misspeculation and rolling back. So in
    our semantics once the misspeculation bit is set to true, it will stay set:

\subsection{Selective SLH transformation}



 Now how can we make CCT programs secure against speculative execution
    attacks? It turns out that we can protect such programs against Spectre v1
    by doing only two things:

\begin{itemize}
\item  Keep track of a misspeculation flag using constant-time conditionals;

\item  Use this flag to mask the value of misspeculated public loads.

\end{itemize}


    We implement this as a \textit{Selective Speculative Load Hardening} (SLH)
    transformation that we will show enforces speculative constant-time security
    for all CCT programs.


    This SLH transformation is ``selective'', since it only masks \textit{public} loads.
    A non-selective SLH transformation was invented in LLVM, but what they
    implement is anyway much more complicated.
